### Review — 2026-10-03 — blocker (MOD-005 beta-video: HDR overexposure on physical-device playback persists after `preferredAssetRepresentationMode.Current` applied to the picker — diagnosis + fix-direction decision)

**Context**: Engineer escalated a persisting cross-module-grade playback defect from Rev 24 Phase 1 device testing. Beta videos look blown-out/overexposed when played back in-app after upload, even after the picker was set to `UIImagePickerPreferredAssetRepresentationMode.Current`. No compression is applied (react-native-compressor removed in Rev 24). Advisory only — I read `BetaVideoUploader.tsx` (picker config, lines 145–159), `beta-video-service.ts` (upload path: `new ExpoFile(uri).bytes()` → `Uint8Array` → single-POST `.upload({ contentType })`, lines 90–169, with `contentType` set to `video/quicktime` or `video/mp4` only), `BetaVideoPlayer.tsx` (`useVideoPlayer` + `VideoView`, no HDR/color config), `BetaVideoReelViewer.tsx` (`useVideoPlayer(null)` + `replaceAsync`, no HDR/color config), and the PRD (§4 tech-stack video-hosting row, §5 architecture Storage bullet, US-004 known-trade-off note, AC-031/035). I also confirmed via grep that there is **no HDR/Dolby/colorspace/videoRange configuration anywhere in `src/`, `app.json`, or any app config** — both the picker and the player are running at their SDK defaults. No source, migration, spec, or PRD files changed here. **Decision input, not a directive — human + PM decide; a Phase-1 code fix routes to `engineer-mod-beta-video`; any convention/AC/PRD reconciliation routes PM → Doc-Sync.**

**Direct answers to the five questions:**

**Q1 — Is `PHPickerConfigurationAssetRepresentationMode.current` guaranteed to prevent transcoding on iOS 17+, or a best-effort hint?**
- It is a **best-effort hint, not a guarantee**. `.current` asks PHPicker to return the asset's *current* representation without generating a new one, which for most assets avoids transcoding. But it is not a contract that the bytes you receive are byte-identical to the capture original. For Dolby Vision (profile 8.4) content especially, when iOS exports/copies the PHAsset into the app sandbox it can still perform a representation conversion, and the exact behavior varies by iOS point-release and by how the clip was captured (HDR10 vs Dolby Vision vs HLG). So `.current` was the correct thing to set, but it cannot be relied on to produce a guaranteed-untouched HDR file. This means cause #1 (picker-side tone-mapping during copy) is *possible but not confirmable from code alone* — it has to be distinguished by inspecting the actual uploaded bytes (see Q4).

**Q2 — Does expo-video's `VideoView` support Dolby Vision / HDR playback natively via AVPlayer on iOS, or does it need explicit configuration?**
- This is the **most likely root cause** and the cheapest thing to prove. expo-video wraps `AVPlayer`/`AVPlayerLayer`. AVPlayer *can* render HDR/Dolby Vision on capable iOS hardware, but correct HDR rendering depends on the hosting layer's color/EDR configuration and on the device actually being an HDR-capable display in an HDR-enabled context. expo-video (both in the inline `BetaVideoPlayer` and the fullscreen `BetaVideoReelViewer`) sets **no** HDR/EDR/color-space properties and exposes no such prop in this codebase — it is running at the component default. A common and exact match for the reported symptom is: the file in storage is genuinely HDR, but the player/host layer is in an SDR path and tone-maps (or fails to tone-map) the HDR signal incorrectly, producing a washed-out / blown-out look. **The symptom "looks overexposed on playback" is the classic signature of an HDR signal rendered through an SDR-assuming path, far more than it is the signature of a file that was actually brightened on disk.** That points at the player, not the upload.

**Q3 — Is Supabase Storage likely to strip HDR metadata (Dolby Vision SEI NAL units) from an HEVC upload?**
- **No — this is the least likely cause and can be effectively ruled out by how the upload path is written.** The upload is a raw byte copy: `new ExpoFile(uri).bytes()` returns a `Uint8Array` of the file as-is, and `.upload(fileName, uint8Array, { contentType })` sends those exact bytes. Supabase Storage is object storage — it stores and serves the bytes verbatim; it does not demux/remux HEVC or touch SEI NAL units, and a signed-URL GET returns the same bytes. The only metadata concern on retrieval is the HTTP `Content-Type` (set correctly to `video/quicktime`/`video/mp4`), which does not alter the elementary stream. So if the bytes going *in* are HDR, the bytes coming *out* are the same HDR. This cause only becomes real if the bytes were already degraded before upload (i.e. cause #1), which is upstream of storage.

**Q4 — Recommended next diagnostic step: how to tell "file in storage is still HDR" vs "player renders it wrong"?**
- **Pull the actual uploaded object out of Supabase Storage and inspect it off-device — this is the single highest-information step and it definitively splits cause #1/#3 from cause #2.** Concretely: download the stored object (via the Supabase dashboard or a signed URL) to a Mac and run `ffprobe` / `mediainfo` on it, checking `color_transfer` / `color_primaries` / `color_space` and whether Dolby Vision configuration/SEI is present (look for `smpte2084`/`arib-std-b67` transfer, `bt2020` primaries, and any `dv` / Dolby Vision tags). Compare those fields against the **original clip still in the Photos library** (AirDrop the original to the Mac and probe it too). Two outcomes, each of which chooses the fix:
  - **(a) Stored file is still HDR (matches the original's HDR fields)** → upload is fine; the defect is in playback. The fix is player-side (cause #2), and Supabase/upload are exonerated. This is the outcome I expect.
  - **(b) Stored file is already SDR / tone-mapped (HDR fields stripped or converted vs the original)** → the picker copy (cause #1) degraded it despite `.current`; no player change will help, and the only Phase-1 levers are picker-level (which iOS does not fully expose) or accepting the limitation (Q5).
- Running this probe before writing any fix is the same discipline as the "strong-WiFi distinguishing test" from the 2026-10-03 TLS review: do not build a fix on an unconfirmed cause.

**Q5 — Accept as a known Phase-1 limitation with a user-facing note, or is there a viable client-side fix?**
- **Decision input, not my call — but my recommendation: do the free diagnostic (Q4) first; the answer determines whether there is a viable fix at all.**
  - If Q4 → outcome (a) (player-side): there may be a low-cost, in-scope player fix — forcing a defined behavior on the AVPlayer host layer so HDR is tone-mapped to SDR correctly rather than blown out. This would be a MOD-005 change to `BetaVideoPlayer` and `BetaVideoReelViewer`, but **only if expo-video 57 actually exposes the relevant color/EDR control**; this codebase sets none today and I could not confirm from the code that such a prop exists in this expo-video version. If the control is not exposed, a player-side fix is not viable in Phase 1 without ejecting further into native, which is out of scope.
  - If Q4 → outcome (b) (picker-side degradation): there is **no reliable client-side fix in Phase 1** — `.current` is already the strongest picker lever and it is only a hint.
  - **In either case, accepting it as a known Phase-1 limitation is defensible and consistent with the PRD**, which already documents Phase 1 as no-transcode and explicitly assigns HDR→SDR tone-mapping to the Phase 2 AWS MediaConvert pipeline (§4 video-hosting row, §7 Phase 2 scope). A user-facing note ("for best results, record in a non-HDR/SDR mode, or disable HDR video in iOS Camera settings") is a reasonable Phase-1 mitigation. But note: that note is **user-facing product copy and a scope statement — it is a PRD/AC change (likely a new AC under US-004 alongside the existing known-trade-off note), not an engineer code decision.** Route it PM → Doc-Sync, do not let it land as an ad-hoc string.

**Concerns** (must address before proceeding):
- **Concern 1 — Run the Q4 off-device `ffprobe`/`mediainfo` comparison before writing any fix. This gates the entire decision.** The three remaining causes (picker-copy #1, player #2, storage #3) have completely different fixes, and storage (#3) is already near-eliminated by the raw-byte upload path. Probing the stored object vs the original is the one test that separates "upload degraded it" from "player renders it wrong," and it costs nothing. Choosing a player fix or a user-note before this probe is guessing.
- **Concern 2 — Do not attempt a player-side HDR fix until it is confirmed that expo-video 57 exposes a color/EDR control.** This codebase sets no HDR/color property on `VideoView` in either `BetaVideoPlayer` or `BetaVideoReelViewer`, and I could not confirm from the code that expo-video 57 surfaces such a prop. If it does not, a correct player-side fix requires native work that is out of Phase-1 scope — in which case the realistic Phase-1 outcome is the known-limitation + user-note path (Q5), not a code fix. The engineer should confirm the prop exists against the installed expo-video version before committing to a player fix.
- **Concern 3 — A user-facing "disable HDR" note is a PRD/AC change, not a code string. Route it PM → Doc-Sync.** It is product scope copy that belongs with the existing US-004 known-trade-off note, and it should be worded/localized (EN + zh-TW) and tracked as an AC so QA can verify it. Dropping it directly into an i18n catalog as an untracked string would bypass the PRD and the bilingual-copy convention.

**Recommendations** (suggested improvements):
- **Recommend the device-validation bar already set on 2026-10-02 be applied here too**: whatever fix (or no-fix) is chosen, the resolution must be validated on a physical HDR-capable iPhone with a real Dolby-Vision/HDR clip, not only in simulator or on an SDR asset — the whole class of bug is invisible on SDR test media.
- **If the probe shows the stored file is HDR and no player control is available**, the cleanest Phase-1 resolution is: (1) PM adds a user-facing AC under US-004 for the "record in SDR for best results" guidance (EN + zh-TW), (2) the existing US-004 known-trade-off note is extended to name HDR overexposure explicitly as a Phase-1 limitation resolved by the Phase-2 MediaConvert HDR→SDR step. This keeps the limitation documented and testable rather than silent.

**Approved**:
- The picker already sets `preferredAssetRepresentationMode.Current` — the correct and strongest available picker-level lever; no change needed there regardless of outcome.
- The upload path (`File.bytes()` → `Uint8Array` → `.upload({ contentType })`) is a verbatim byte copy and correctly does not transcode or touch the elementary stream — consistent with the Rev 24 no-compression/no-re-encode model (AC-031). Supabase Storage is effectively ruled out as the cause.
- Assigning HDR→SDR tone-mapping to the Phase-2 AWS MediaConvert pipeline (not Phase 1) is sound and already in the PRD; a Phase-1 known-limitation posture is architecturally consistent with that.

### Review — 2026-10-03 — blocker (MOD-005 beta-video: TLS upload failure "StorageUnknownError: fetch failed" on physical device during Rev 24 Phase 1 human QA — fix-direction decision A vs B vs C)

**Context**: Engineer escalated a cross-module-blocker-grade failure from Rev 24 Phase 1 human QA: a physical-device beta-video upload failed with `StorageUnknownError: fetch failed: UnexpectedException: TLS錯誤導致安全連線失敗 (at ExpoModulesCore/Promise.swift:56)`. Engineer's full diagnosis is in `project-planning/modules/mod-beta-video/status.md` (Investigation entry, 2026-10-03). The failure originates in iOS URLSession (native TLS), not a Supabase HTTP error response — the connection dropped before any HTTP response arrived. Rev 24 removed compression, so files are now native HEVC (20–44 MB vs the former ~15 MB compressed). Advisory only — I read the engineer's diagnosis, `beta-video-service.ts` (the `File.bytes()` → `Uint8Array` → single-POST `.upload()` path, lines 90–169), the `production.md` "File Upload to Supabase Storage" convention (line 139) and Video Pipeline Conventions (125–137), the relevant ACs (AC-031/035/036/169, Rev 24), `package.json`, and — critically — the **actually-installed** `@supabase/storage-js` dist + type declarations. No source, migration, spec, or PRD files changed here. **Decision input, not a directive — human + PM decide; the Phase-1 code fix routes to `engineer-mod-beta-video`; any convention/AC reconciliation routes PM → Doc-Sync.**

**A material correction to one premise of the diagnosis (does not change the ranking of root causes, but it changes the fix menu):** The diagnosis states `@supabase/storage-js` v2.49 has no first-class TUS/signed-upload API and that `signal`/timeout is unavailable. The version range in `package.json` is `^2.49.4`, but the **resolved installed version is `@supabase/storage-js@2.109.0`** (`node_modules/@supabase/storage-js/package.json`). That is a much newer client and it changes what is possible without a new dependency:
- It exposes **`createSignedUploadUrl(path)`** and **`uploadToSignedUrl(path, token, body, fileOptions)`** (confirmed in `dist/index.d.cts` lines 938/985 and `dist/index.cjs`). This is a first-class Supabase resumable/direct-upload primitive that does NOT require `tus-js-client`.
- It accepts **`ReadableStream` / Node-stream bodies with automatic `duplex: 'half'`** in `uploadOrUpdate` (`dist/index.cjs` line ~623): a streamed body avoids materialising the whole file as one `Uint8Array` — directly relevant to cause #2 (memory pressure).
- **BUT** I confirmed the diagnosis is still correct on one key point: the `upload()` / `uploadToSignedUrl()` **`FileOptions` interface does NOT carry a `signal` field** (`dist/index.d.cts` lines 245–270). The `signal?: AbortSignal` that exists in the client (line 367, `FetchParameters`) is wired only into download/list/remove, NOT the upload path. So a *clean, first-class* `AbortSignal` on `.upload()` is still not available even on 2.109.0 — Option A's timeout must be implemented by wrapping the upload promise (e.g. `Promise.race` against a timeout, or by passing a custom `fetch` to the Supabase client that injects an `AbortController`), not by a documented `.upload({ signal })` option.

**Direct answers to the four questions:**

**Q1 — Is the `File.bytes()` → `Uint8Array` constraint revisable for TUS streaming, or does the prior incident that motivated it apply to TUS too?**
- The prior incident (production.md line 139) was specifically about **`fetch(uri) + response.blob()`**: in Expo SDK 57+, `Response.blob()` routes bytes through the native blob store via base64, causing silent failures. That failure mode is about `blob()`, **not** about buffered-vs-streamed uploads in general. A TUS/streaming path that reads the file via `expo-file-system` (File handle / `ReadableStream`) and streams it does **not** reinvoke the `blob()` base64 path, so the prior incident does **not** automatically condemn it.
- **However**, `File.bytes()` → `Uint8Array` is the *only* upload path that is currently proven on this project's device matrix. TUS streaming from a file URI in Expo bare on iOS is **unproven here** and carries its own RN-fetch-streaming caveats (RN's `fetch` has historically not supported request-body streaming without `duplex`/polyfills; Supabase's own TUS guidance for RN uses `tus-js-client` with a file-system shim, not the storage-js streaming body). So: the constraint is *revisable in principle*, but revising it means swapping a proven path for an unproven one. That is a real risk, not a free win — it must be a deliberate, PM-logged convention amendment, not a silent code change, and it must be device-validated before QA (same bar as the HDR-HEVC device-validation condition from the 2026-10-02 sign-off).

**Q2 — Is `tus-js-client` an acceptable new dependency in Expo bare (iOS-only)? Any known compatibility issues?**
- `tus-js-client` is pure JS (no native module), so it does not add a config-plugin/pod and will not by itself break an Expo bare iOS build — that part is low-risk. **The real compatibility issue is file access, not the TUS protocol:** `tus-js-client` in React Native needs a way to read chunks from a local file URI. The standard approach is its `fileReader`/`urlStorage` shims plus a file-system bridge; the project already depends on `expo-file-system` (the `File` class), which can supply bytes, but wiring `tus-js-client`'s chunked reader to `expo-file-system` is **custom integration code that is unproven on this project** and would need its own tests + device validation. Net: the *dependency* is acceptable; the *integration* is non-trivial and unproven. And note — given the 2.109.0 finding above, **you likely do not need `tus-js-client` at all**: `createSignedUploadUrl` + `uploadToSignedUrl` is Supabase's own resumable primitive and keeps you inside the already-approved storage-js client.

**Q3 — Concrete recommendation: A, B, C, or hybrid?**
- **Recommended: a phased hybrid — C-as-a-test first (mandatory, costs nothing), then A as the Phase-1 fix, with B explicitly deferred to the Phase-2 AWS pipeline. Do NOT adopt B (TUS / new dependency / convention amendment) as a Phase-1 fix.** Rationale below.

**Q4 — Should the human re-test on strong WiFi first before any code change, to rule out cause #3?**
- **Yes — unconditionally, and before any code is written.** This is the cheapest, highest-information step available and it is a prerequisite for choosing correctly between the directions. See Concern 1.

**Concerns** (must address before proceeding):
- **Concern 1 — Run the distinguishing test before writing any fix. This gates everything else.** The engineer's own cause #3 is untested, and the three fix directions have very different costs. Have the human reproduce on a **strong, stable WiFi** connection with a **known ~20 MB `.mov`** and separately with a **~40 MB** file. (a) If it passes reliably on strong WiFi and only fails on cellular/weak WiFi → the dominant driver is environmental (cause #3); the right Phase-1 fix is the *smallest* one (Option A: timeout + retry + a clear "weak connection" error), not a structural rebuild. (b) If it **fails on strong WiFi too**, especially at the 40 MB end → causes #1/#2 are confirmed structural and the case for a streamed/resumable path (B, or the storage-js signed-upload variant) strengthens. Choosing B before running this test is building a four-plus-hour structural change on an unconfirmed hypothesis.
- **Concern 2 — Option A as specified cannot use a clean `.upload({ signal })` — that option does not exist even on the installed 2.109.0 client (confirmed above).** If PM/human pick A, the engineer must implement the timeout by wrapping the upload promise (timeout race that rejects with a user-facing "upload timed out — check your connection" message) and/or by supplying a custom `fetch` to the Supabase client constructor that injects an `AbortController`. The "retry-once" leg is fine and additive. This is a spec detail the engineer needs stated explicitly so they don't go looking for a `signal` option that isn't on the upload path.
- **Concern 3 — Option B is a convention amendment AND an unproven-path swap AND (if `tus-js-client`) a new-dependency decision — three gated changes, none of which belong in an urgent QA-unblock.** If the data from Concern 1 ends up justifying a structural path, the *lower-risk structural option is Supabase's own `createSignedUploadUrl`/`uploadToSignedUrl` on the already-installed 2.109.0 client* — it stays inside the approved client, needs no new dependency, and is a smaller convention delta than TUS. Reserve full `tus-js-client` TUS for the Phase-2 AWS work (where the ingest path is being rebuilt anyway), not Phase 1.
- **Concern 4 — the memory-pressure cause (#2) is real and is NOT addressed by Option A.** `File.bytes()` materialising a 44 MB `Uint8Array` on a memory-constrained device is a legitimate structural weakness. If strong-WiFi testing shows failures that correlate with large files rather than weak network, A will reduce but not eliminate them. That is the honest limit of A, and PM should hear it when weighing A vs a streamed path.

**Recommendations** (advisory — human/PM decide; code → `engineer-mod-beta-video`, AC/convention → PM → Doc-Sync):
- **Step 1 (now, no code): run the Concern-1 distinguishing test.** Record the result (strong-WiFi 20 MB, strong-WiFi 40 MB, cellular 20 MB) in the module status. This is the single most valuable next action and it is free.
- **Step 2 (Phase-1 fix, after Step 1): implement Option A** — a bounded upload timeout (wrap the promise per Concern 2; ~120 s is reasonable but set it from the slowest acceptable connection, not a guess) + a single automatic retry + a distinct, actionable bilingual error for the timeout/connection case ("連線逾時，請確認網路後再試 / Upload timed out — check your connection and try again"), separate from the existing size/413 messages. This is low-risk, no new dependency, no convention change, and it directly addresses the most-likely structural driver (cause #1) and the environmental driver (cause #3). It is the correct Phase-1 move regardless of the test outcome — even a structural fix should have a timeout.
- **Step 3 (only if Step 1 shows large-file failures persist on strong WiFi): evaluate the storage-js `createSignedUploadUrl` + `uploadToSignedUrl` streamed path as a targeted follow-up** (not `tus-js-client`). This stays inside the approved client and is a smaller convention amendment than TUS. Route it as its own PM-logged change → Doc-Sync convention update → engineer, with mandatory device validation before QA. Do not bundle it into the urgent unblock.
- **Do NOT choose Option C as the resolution.** C ("wait for Phase 2") is only acceptable as the *diagnostic framing* (confirm cause #3), never as the fix — shipping Phase-1 TestFlight with a known physical-device upload failure and no timeout/retry is not acceptable, and the Phase-2 AWS pipeline is a future plan (approved in principle, build-time re-review per the 2026-10-02 sign-off), not an imminent deliverable that can absorb this.
- **Do NOT adopt Option B (`tus-js-client` + convention amendment) as the Phase-1 fix.** Three gated changes on an urgent unblock, on an unproven path, when a zero-dependency option (A) plus a smaller same-client structural fallback (Step 3) exist.

**Approved** (sound as-is):
- **The engineer's root-cause analysis and ranking** (TLS mid-transfer drop #1 / memory pressure #2 / transient network #3) — technically sound; the `StorageUnknownError`-on-`fetch`-throw reasoning is correct, and the "no `AbortSignal` on the upload path" finding holds even on the installed 2.109.0 client.
- **Recording the investigation without implementing a fix** — correct call; this decision needed Tech Lead + PM/human input before code.
- **The distinguishing-test design** (strong-WiFi, known file size) — exactly the right experiment; make it the mandatory first step.

**Proposed Shared Conventions** (for Doc-Sync to carry into production.md, only if human/PM approve):
- **All Supabase Storage uploads must enforce a client-side upload timeout and a single automatic retry.** A raw `supabase.storage.*.upload()` call has no timeout and no retry; on iOS a large body over a weak connection surfaces as an opaque `StorageUnknownError: fetch failed` (native TLS drop). Uploads must be wrapped with a bounded timeout (reject with a distinct, actionable "connection/timeout" error, kept separate from size/413 errors) and retried once before surfacing failure to the user. (Note for the engineer: the installed `@supabase/storage-js` 2.109.0 does **not** expose a `signal` option on the upload `FileOptions` — the timeout must be a promise wrapper or a custom `fetch` injected at client construction, not a `.upload({ signal })` call.)
- **(Documentation correction, independent of the fix choice):** the installed `@supabase/storage-js` is **2.109.0**, not the `^2.49.4` floor implied by `package.json`. It supports `createSignedUploadUrl` / `uploadToSignedUrl` (resumable/direct upload) and `ReadableStream` bodies with `duplex: 'half'`. Any future "we need resumable/streamed uploads" work should prefer these first-class storage-js primitives over adding `tus-js-client`, keeping the project inside the already-approved client. (This supersedes the diagnosis's "v2.49 has no TUS API" premise.)

### Review — 2026-10-02 — change (MOD-005 beta-video: FINAL SIGN-OFF on the consolidated two-phase plan — Phase 1 no-compression 45 MB gate; Phase 2 AWS S3 + CloudFront + MediaConvert)

**Context**: Human requested a final architectural sign-off on the *consolidated* beta-video plan before it goes to PM for AC revisions. This plan is the same Phase-1 no-compression pivot I reviewed in the 2026-10-01 entry immediately below (upfront `new ExpoFile(asset.uri).size` gate at 45 MB, reject-if-over, fail-closed on `size === 0`, remove `react-native-compressor` entirely, drop the "Compressing…" overlay phase, new 800 MB free-tier watch threshold), now written up with two new elements I had not reviewed: (a) a *finalized* Phase-2 architecture that is **AWS S3 + CloudFront + MediaConvert** rather than the Cloudflare Stream the PRD §4/production.md line 321 names as the migration target, and (b) four explicit questions. Advisory only — I re-read `BetaVideoUploader.tsx`, `beta-video-service.ts`, `production.md` Video Pipeline Conventions (125–131) + migration trigger (321), the PRD ACs (AC-030/031/032/035/036/169), `package.json` (expo `~57.0.24`, expo-video `~57.0.5`, react-native-compressor `^1.16.0`, expo-file-system `File` class imported in shipped code), and my four prior 2026-10-01 beta-video reviews below. No source, migration, spec, or PRD files changed here. **Decision input, not a directive — human + PM decide; Phase-1 implementation routes to `engineer-mod-beta-video`, AC/convention reconciliations route PM → Doc-Sync.**

**SIGN-OFF: APPROVED, conditional.** The plan is technically sound. Phase 1 is approved exactly as I detailed in the 2026-10-01 entry below — **all of that still stands and is not repeated here**; read that entry for the Phase-1 concerns (AC-031/AC-169 rewrite, container-only codec enforcement, fail-closed size read, HDR HEVC device validation, dependency removal, overlay simplification). The three conditions below are the gate on "approved." This sign-off adds the Phase-2 AWS verdict and answers the four questions.

**Direct answers to the four questions:**

**Q1 — Architectural objections to Phase 1 or Phase 2?**
- **Phase 1: none beyond the 2026-10-01 entry.** The reject-at-the-gate model is the right Phase-1 choice. No new objection.
- **Phase 2: one documentation objection, no architectural objection.** AWS S3 + CloudFront + MediaConvert is a completely sound, industry-standard managed video pipeline and will do exactly what the plan says (presigned-URL ingest → S3 event → Lambda → MediaConvert HDR→SDR + H.264 → CloudFront). **BUT it is NOT the migration target the PRD and production.md currently name — both say Cloudflare Stream** (PRD §4 line 25, production.md line 127/321). You cannot sign off a Phase-2 plan that silently contradicts the documented target. This is the same anti-pattern I flagged for AC-031 in Phase 1: ship-the-plan-leave-the-doc-wrong. **Condition 2 below covers this** — the migration-target change (Cloudflare Stream → AWS S3/CloudFront/MediaConvert) must be an explicit PM-logged decision → Doc-Sync into production.md, not an implicit drift. Note also that adopting the AWS stack *and* keeping Supabase (even free tier) means the project runs two clouds — fine operationally, but it's a real decision (two billing accounts, two IAM/secrets surfaces, cross-cloud latency on the Edge-Function→S3 presign hop) that should be recorded, not assumed.

**Q2 — Is the presigned-S3-URL-via-Supabase-Edge-Function pattern the right integration, or is there something simpler/safer that keeps Supabase Auth as the identity layer?**
- **Yes, it is the correct and safest pattern — it is the industry-standard way to let a mobile client upload to S3 while a non-AWS identity provider (Supabase Auth) stays the source of truth. Keep it.** The flow is right: client sends its Supabase JWT to an Edge Function → the Edge Function verifies the JWT (Supabase Auth stays the identity layer) → the Edge Function holds the *only* AWS credentials (an IAM user/role scoped to `s3:PutObject` on one prefix) and mints a short-lived presigned `PUT` URL → client uploads bytes directly to S3. The mobile app never sees an AWS credential. This is exactly how you bridge a non-Cognito identity layer to S3.
- **Specific hardening requirements (make these spec/convention, not afterthoughts):**
  - **Scope the presigned URL tightly**: single `PUT`, short expiry (5 min), a server-chosen key (`{user_id}/{uuid}.mov`) so the client cannot choose arbitrary paths, and — critically — use a **presigned POST with a `content-length-range` policy condition** (or enforce size at the S3-event/Lambda stage) so a client cannot upload a 2 GB file through a presigned `PUT` (a plain presigned PUT cannot cap size; presigned POST can). Without this, the 45 MB gate is purely client-side on the Phase-2 path and trivially bypassed.
  - **The IAM principal behind the Edge Function must be least-privilege**: `s3:PutObject` on the ingest prefix only — no list, no get, no delete, no other buckets. Its credentials live in Supabase Edge Function secrets (env), never in the client bundle, never in the repo. (Standard no-`.env`-in-git rule applies.)
  - **Keep Supabase Auth as identity — do NOT introduce Cognito.** The invocation already assumes this; I'm confirming it's the right call. Adding Cognito to get native S3 auth would duplicate your identity layer and is strictly worse than the Edge-Function-presign bridge for this use case.
- **Simpler alternatives and why they're worse:** (a) *Cloudflare Stream direct-creator-upload* is arguably even simpler (Stream mints a one-time upload URL via its own API; no Lambda/MediaConvert/S3-event wiring to build) and is the already-documented target — if the only goal is "get video off Supabase with transcode + CDN," Stream is less infrastructure to own than the S3+Lambda+MediaConvert+CloudFront four-service chain. That's not an objection to AWS, it's a reminder that the plan is choosing *more* moving parts than the documented alternative, so the choice should be deliberate (see Condition 2). (b) *Client uploads directly to S3 with embedded credentials* — never; disqualified. (c) *Route bytes through the Edge Function itself* (client → Edge Function → S3) — disqualified: Edge Functions have request-size/time limits unfit for 100 MB video, and it wastes a hop. Presigned-URL direct-to-S3 is correct.

**Q3 — Are the Phase-2 cost estimates in the right ballpark?**
- **Directionally yes, with two caveats that make the real bill modestly higher than the ~$13–15/mo quoted.** The individual line items are reasonable for the stated scale:
  - **S3 50 GB ≈ $1.15/mo** — correct ($0.023/GB-mo, ap-northeast-1/ap-southeast). Fine.
  - **MediaConvert 200 × 30 s HD ≈ $0.75/mo** — in the right range but likely **light**. MediaConvert basic-tier HD (<30fps) is ~$0.0075–$0.015 per output-minute depending on region/features; 200 × 0.5 min = 100 output-minutes → ~$0.75–$1.50 for a *single* output rendition. The plan says "H.264 720p/1080p output" — if you emit **both** a 720p and a 1080p rendition per video (sensible for adaptive delivery) you double the output-minutes, and HDR→SDR tone-mapping can push you into the **professional tier** (~2× basic). Realistic: **$1.50–$3/mo**, not $0.75. Still trivial.
  - **CloudFront 100 GB (Asia-Pacific) ≈ $11/mo** — this is the dominant cost and it's **plausibly understated**. AP (Japan/HK/SG) CloudFront egress is ~$0.114–$0.140/GB, so 100 GB ≈ **$11.4–$14/mo** for egress alone, plus per-10k-request charges. If Taiwan traffic routes through the Japan edge at the higher AP tier, call it **$12–$16/mo**.
  - **Missing line items:** Lambda invocations (negligible, pennies), S3 `PUT`/`GET` requests (negligible at this scale), MediaConvert→S3 `PUT` of outputs (negligible), and — the one that can surprise you — **CloudFront requests + any S3-to-CloudFront origin fetches** (small but non-zero).
- **Net realistic Phase-2 bill at the stated scale: ~$16–$22/mo**, versus the quoted $13–15. Same order of magnitude — the estimate is *fine for a go/no-go decision* — but **note it crosses the PRD's own $25 migration-trigger line's spirit**: the whole reason this is "Phase 2" is that the free tier ran out, and the AWS bill lands in the ~$20/mo range, which is the same ballpark as just buying **Supabase Pro ($25/mo, 100 GB, 200 MB/file)** and changing *nothing* in the code. That is the real decision Q3 should surface for PM: **for ~$25/mo Supabase Pro you keep one cloud, one identity layer, one upload path, and zero new infra; for ~$16–22/mo AWS you get a purpose-built transcode+CDN pipeline but take on a second cloud and a four-service integration to build and operate.** At single-gym/single-city Phase-1→early-Phase-2 scale, Supabase Pro is very likely the better dollar-for-effort move and defers the AWS build until real delivery volume (where CloudFront's CDN economics actually win) justifies it. This is a product/ops call for the human + PM, not mine — flagging it, not deciding it.

**Q4 — Proposed Shared Conventions for production.md?**
- See the dedicated block at the end of this entry. In short: (1) carry forward the four Phase-1 no-compression conventions from the 2026-10-01 entry below (they already cover the Phase-1 gate, deferral, 1 GB ceiling, and device-validation); (2) add one Phase-2 convention recording that the migration target is now AWS S3 + CloudFront + MediaConvert (superseding Cloudflare Stream) and that client→S3 ingest uses a Supabase-Edge-Function-minted, size-capped, short-lived presigned upload with Supabase Auth as the identity layer and least-privilege IAM behind the function.

**Concerns** (must address before proceeding — these are the conditions on the conditional approval):
- **Condition 1 (Phase 1, unchanged from 2026-10-01): AC-031 and AC-169 must be rewritten before/with the code change, and `react-native-compressor` removed entirely.** AC-031 currently *mandates* compression; AC-169 is defined as a *post-compression* guard. Shipping the no-compression code without revising both leaves two Phase-1 ACs in a permanent fail state. Also reconcile production.md line 127 (which still "recommends `react-native-compressor`") and the 45 MB-gate/60 s-cap tension (AC-030/US-004 promise "≤60 s" but the gate rejects most 30–60 s raw HEVC clips — a conscious PM product call, with clear actionable bilingual copy, "請修剪影片 / trim your video" preferred over "compress"). All routes PM → Doc-Sync. Full detail in the 2026-10-01 entry below.
- **Condition 2 (Phase 2): the migration-target change from Cloudflare Stream to AWS S3 + CloudFront + MediaConvert must be an explicit PM-logged decision, synced into production.md line 127/321 and PRD §4 line 25, NOT an implicit drift.** The docs currently name Cloudflare Stream. Approving the AWS plan means consciously overriding the documented target; record *why* (and record that the project will run two clouds). Until this is logged and synced, production.md and the plan disagree — the exact code/doc-divergence anti-pattern this framework exists to prevent.
- **Condition 3 (Phase 2 security, non-negotiable when Phase 2 is built): the presigned-upload path must enforce an upload size cap server-side (presigned POST `content-length-range`, or Lambda/S3-event validation), use least-privilege IAM (`s3:PutObject` on the ingest prefix only), short expiry (≤5 min), and a server-chosen object key.** A plain presigned `PUT` cannot cap size — without this the 45 MB gate becomes client-side-only and bypassable on the Phase-2 path, re-opening the exact storage-blowout risk Phase 1 closed. This is a spec requirement for the Phase-2 MOD-005 work, not optional.

**Recommendations** (advisory — human/PM decide; code → `engineer-mod-beta-video`, AC/convention → PM → Doc-Sync):
- **Sign off Phase 1 and proceed** via the PM→Doc-Sync→Engineer→QA sequence spelled out in the 2026-10-01 entry below. Phase 1 is the only thing that needs to be *built now*; Phase 2 is a documented future plan, not an immediate implementation.
- **Before committing to the AWS Phase-2 build, have PM explicitly weigh Supabase Pro (~$25/mo, zero new infra, one cloud) against AWS S3/CloudFront/MediaConvert (~$16–22/mo, four new services, two clouds) (Q3).** At the plan's stated early scale, Supabase Pro is likely the better dollar-for-effort move and defers the AWS integration until delivery volume justifies CloudFront's CDN economics. Record whichever is chosen and why.
- **Treat the Phase-2 design as "approved in principle, re-reviewed at build time."** When Phase 2 is actually scheduled, re-invoke Tech Lead for a detailed review of the presigned-upload Edge Function, the IAM policy, the MediaConvert job template (HDR→SDR tone-map settings, rendition count), and the `beta_videos.video_url` CloudFront-URL migration (existing rows hold Supabase Storage paths; a backfill/dual-read strategy is needed). Those details are out of scope for a pre-AC-revision sign-off.
- **Keep everything approved in the 2026-10-01 entry**: 45 MB decimal gate, fail-closed-on-unknown-size, HDR HEVC device validation, dependency removal, overlay simplification, 800 MB free-tier watch threshold.

**Approved** (sound as-is):
- **Phase 1 no-compression 45 MB-gate architecture** — approved (conditions 1 above + all of the 2026-10-01 entry).
- **Phase 2 AWS S3 + CloudFront + MediaConvert pipeline, in principle** — a sound, standard managed video architecture; approved subject to conditions 2 and 3 and a build-time re-review.
- **The presigned-S3-URL-via-Supabase-Edge-Function integration pattern with Supabase Auth as the identity layer (Q2)** — this is the correct and safest pattern; no simpler/safer alternative that keeps Supabase Auth; confirmed. (Hardening per Condition 3.)
- **The "only two functions change" blast-radius claim** — correct: `uploadBetaVideo` (swap Supabase Storage upload for presigned-S3 PUT) and `getVideoSignedUrl` (return CloudFront URL) are the only Phase-2 service-layer changes, because Postgres/Auth/RLS/PostREST/other-storage all stay on Supabase. The `beta_videos.video_url` semantics change (storage path → CloudFront URL) is the one data-model wrinkle to plan for (existing-row backfill).
- **The cost estimates as a go/no-go input** — right order of magnitude (~$16–22/mo realistic vs. ~$13–15 quoted); good enough to decide on.

**Proposed Shared Conventions** (for Doc-Sync to carry into production.md, only if human/PM approve):
- **Carry forward the four Phase-1 no-compression conventions from the 2026-10-01 entry below** (upfront 45 MB decimal source-size gate, fail-closed; client compression + HDR tone-map deferred to the server-side migration; 1 GB free-tier ceiling with an 800 MB watch threshold; native HDR HEVC playback verified on a physical device before QA). These supersede the compression conventions from the three earlier reviews.
- **Phase-2 video migration target is AWS S3 (ingest/storage) + AWS MediaConvert (HDR→SDR tone-map + H.264 transcode) + AWS CloudFront (CDN delivery), superseding the previously-documented Cloudflare Stream target** (PRD §4 line 25 / production.md line 127, 321). Supabase remains the system of record for all Postgres data, Auth, RLS, PostgREST, and non-video storage; only the beta-video ingest path and the `beta_videos.video_url` delivery URL move to AWS. The project consequently runs two clouds in Phase 2 — a deliberate, PM-logged trade-off against staying single-cloud on Supabase Pro.
- **Phase-2 client→S3 ingest uses a Supabase-Edge-Function-minted presigned upload**: the Edge Function verifies the caller's Supabase JWT (Supabase Auth stays the identity layer — no Cognito), holds the only AWS credentials (least-privilege IAM, `s3:PutObject` on the ingest prefix only, credentials in Edge-Function secrets never in the client or repo), and returns a short-lived (≤5 min), server-keyed, **size-capped** presigned upload (presigned POST `content-length-range` or equivalent S3-event/Lambda size validation) so the 45 MB cap is enforced server-side, not client-only. The client never holds an AWS credential and never chooses its own object key.

### Review — 2026-10-01 — change (MOD-005 beta-video upload: "no-compression" pivot — upfront 45 MB native-size gate, reject-if-over, Phase 2 = Cloudflare Stream)

**Context**: Human asked for a quick architectural review of a *finalized* Phase-1 decision: **remove `react-native-compressor` and the entire compression step** from `BetaVideoUploader.tsx`, replace it with a single upfront native file-size check (`new ExpoFile(asset.uri).size`), upload the original as-is when native size < 45 MB (full native quality, HDR preserved, plays via AVPlayer), and reject immediately with a bilingual error when native size ≥ 45 MB. Phase 2 remains Cloudflare Stream (upload original, server-side HDR→SDR tone-map + CDN). Three questions: (1) technical objections / dependency cleanup, (2) validate the storage-runway math, (3) should a storage-exhaustion threshold be flagged in the PRD. Advisory only — I read `BetaVideoUploader.tsx`, `beta-video-service.ts`, `production.md` Video Pipeline Conventions (lines 125–131) + migration trigger (line 321), the PRD ACs (AC-031/AC-035/AC-169), the three prior 2026-10-01 beta-video reviews below, and `package.json` (`react-native-compressor@^1.16.0`, `expo-video@~57.0.5`, `expo-image-picker@~57.0.19`; note `expo-file-system` is a transitive/aliased dep — the `File` class is imported via `'expo-file-system'` in the shipped code). No source, migration, spec, or PRD files changed here. **This is a decision input, not a directive — the human + PM decide; implementation routes to `engineer-mod-beta-video`, and the AC/convention reconciliations route PM → Doc-Sync.**

**Direct answers to the three questions:**

**Q1 — Technical objections / edge cases / dependency cleanup.** No objection to the *architecture* — a reject-at-the-gate model is simpler, zero device-CPU/battery, zero compress-phase latency, and removes a fragile native transcode path that has caused three separate 413/guard defects on this project. But it is **not** a drop-in: it contradicts shipped ACs and conventions, and it changes user-facing behavior, so it is a PRD-level change, not pure implementation. Specific points:
- **AC-031 must be rewritten or retired — it currently *mandates* client-side compression** ("The system shall run client-side video compression before upload using `react-native-compressor`…"). The no-compression plan puts AC-031 in a permanent fail state. This is the single biggest blocker: you cannot ship this plan and leave AC-031 as written. PM must revise AC-031 (to a "no client-side compression; native size gate instead" requirement) → Doc-Sync into the MOD-005 spec.
- **AC-169 changes meaning and must be rewritten.** Today AC-169 is a *post-compression* guard ("after compression completes … check the compressed file's size"). With no compression step, the guard moves *upfront* and gates the **source** file, with a different message ("影片檔案過大，請壓縮至 45MB 以下" vs. the current "影片壓縮後仍超過上限"). The threshold is still 45 MB decimal (safe margin under the 50 MB bucket), so that constant is fine — but the AC text, the timing ("before upload begins," no longer "after compression"), and the error copy all change. PM → Doc-Sync.
- **AC-035 codec statement becomes load-bearing and is currently only half-enforced.** With compression removed there is **no longer a transcode that normalizes output to H.264/AAC** — whatever the user picked is what gets stored and played. AC-035 already says reject any upload whose video codec is not H.264 **or HEVC** and whose audio is not AAC, and `production.md` line 127 says "Both `.mp4` and `.mov` files are stored as-is — no server-side transcoding" and that HEVC is an accepted *stored* codec. So storing HEVC as-is is already the documented intent — good, the plan is consistent with AC-035's codec allow-list. **BUT** the shipped `validateVideoFormat` (lines 94–118) only checks the **container** (`.mp4`/`.mov` / MIME), never the codec — it cannot, client-side, from `expo-image-picker`. That was tolerable when the compressor forced H.264 output; with no compressor, a `.mov` wrapping a non-H.264/non-HEVC/non-AAC codec would now be stored and could be unplayable. This is a real new edge case the plan must acknowledge: either accept that iPhone-sourced footage is always HEVC/H.264 + AAC (true in practice for Camera-app and Photos-exported clips) and treat the container check as sufficient for Phase 1, or add a codec probe. Recommend: document the assumption explicitly (iPhone camera/library footage is H.264 or HEVC + AAC by construction) and keep the container-only check for Phase 1 — a codec probe is a Phase-2 nicety.
- **HDR/HEVC playback claim needs a device check — do not take "plays correctly via AVPlayer" on faith.** `expo-video@57` (`VideoView`) uses `AVPlayer` under the hood, which *does* decode HEVC and HDR (HLG/HDR10/Dolby Vision) natively on iOS 12+/compatible hardware — so an un-transcoded iPhone HDR HEVC clip will generally play. Two caveats: (a) HDR content on a non-HDR display or in certain inline contexts can look washed-out/over-bright unless tone-mapped — a known iOS inline-playback quirk; (b) the *thumbnail* path: today the code uses the video URI itself as the thumbnail placeholder (AC-031 Phase-1 simplification) — that's unaffected by removing compression, but confirm the grid still renders. **Validate HDR HEVC playback on a physical device** per the project's "verify native behavior on device, not simulator" convention — the simulator's HEVC/HDR path is exactly where this would give a false positive/negative.
- **Dependency cleanup — remove `react-native-compressor` entirely, do not leave it dormant.** It is a native module (iOS AVFoundation, config plugin). Leaving it installed-but-unused still drags it into the iOS native build, app size, and the `npx expo run:ios` compile — dormant native deps are pure liability. Grep confirms it is referenced in exactly three files (`BetaVideoUploader.tsx`, `types.ts` the `'compressing'` phase comment, `__tests__/BetaVideoUploader.test.tsx`). Remove the import + `CompressorVideo.compress(...)` call, drop the package from `package.json` via `npx expo install` tooling (not a hand-edit), and update the two incidental references. Keeping it "dormant" buys nothing and keeps a retired-risk native dep in the build. **If** Phase 2 Cloudflare Stream is the destination (upload original, server transcodes), there is no future client-side compression need at all — so there is no "we'll re-enable it later" argument. Remove it.
- **The AC-036 progress overlay simplifies** — the "Compressing…" phase disappears; the overlay becomes upload-only (0–100%). Minor, but it's a user-visible copy change (the `'compressing'` phase in `types.ts` and the overlay branch at `BetaVideoUploader.tsx` lines 300–303 become dead). Clean it up in the same change.
- **Edge case — the source-size read must fail CLOSED, same rule as today.** The plan's upfront `new ExpoFile(asset.uri).size` must treat `undefined`/`0`/unreadable as a *block*, not a pass (the shipped code already learned this lesson — lines 215–224 fail closed on `size === 0`). Carry that forward: unknown source size → reject with the too-large message, never upload-on-unknown. A gate that passes on unknown size is the exact bug that caused the second 413 review below.
- **Timing edge case — read the size on the *picked asset URI*, which for `expo-image-picker` is a copied temp file; confirm `.size` resolves on it synchronously.** The shipped code already reads `new ExpoFile(asset.uri).size` on the source at line 189–190 as a >300 MB pre-check, so this path is proven to work on the picked URI. Good — the plan reuses an already-working read.

**Q2 — Storage-runway math. Your estimate is directionally right but your per-file average is too low for the new plan, which makes your runway optimistic. Here are corrected numbers.**
- **Critical correction: removing compression changes the file-size distribution entirely.** Your ~20 MB average came from the *compressed* world (2 Mbps/720p → ~15 MB for 60 s). With **no compression**, accepted files are raw iPhone HEVC originals *up to the 45 MB gate*. The probe in the blocker review below measured a real 54.7 s 1080×1920 HEVC clip at **16.6 Mbps → ~111 MB** (rejected by the gate). The *accepted* set is everything the camera produces under 45 MB:
  - A 10 s 1080p HEVC clip at ~16 Mbps ≈ **~20 MB**.
  - A 20 s clip ≈ **~40 MB** — near the gate.
  - A 30 s clip ≈ **~60 MB** — **rejected**. A 60 s clip ≈ **~120 MB** — **rejected**.
  - So the 45 MB gate effectively caps accepted raw HEVC clips at **~22 s of 1080p footage**, and the accepted-set average lands around **25–35 MB**, not 20 MB — because without compression every accepted file sits in the upper-left of the size band (short but high-bitrate), and the gate deliberately admits files right up to 45 MB.
- **If you keep `videoMaxDuration: 60` with NO compression, most 30–60 s iPhone clips will hit the 45 MB reject.** That is a real UX consequence: the feature promises "≤60 s beta" (AC-030, US-004) but the no-compression gate will *reject a large fraction of legitimate 30–60 s clips* because raw HEVC at full bitrate blows past 45 MB well before 60 s. This is the most important finding in Q2: **the no-compression plan and the 60 s duration cap are in tension** — you will be telling users "up to 60 seconds" and then rejecting their 40 s clip for size. Options to resolve: (a) accept the friction and rely on the clear error message (users trim and retry), (b) lower the effective duration expectation, or (c) acknowledge this is exactly what compression *solved* and that removing it trades quality-loss-risk for rejection-friction. This is a product trade-off for PM, not a pure tech call — flagging it, not deciding it.
- **Corrected runway at 1 GB free tier (shared across *all* buckets — avatars, gym photos, route photos, thumbnails all consume the same 1 GB):**
  - At your 20 MB avg: ~50 videos (your number — but too optimistic for the no-compression distribution).
  - At a realistic **30 MB avg** for the no-compression accepted set: **~33 videos** before the 1 GB *total* is exhausted — and that's *before* subtracting avatars/gym/route photos/thumbnails, which are not free. Net video runway is more like **25–30 videos**.
  - Your single-gym scenario (30 routes × 30 users × 2–3 videos) is directionally correct and, if anything, understated: at 30 MB avg, even **35 videos = ~1.05 GB** already exhausts the free tier. The free tier is a **single-gym-single-week** capacity at best under the no-compression plan.
- **Net: the no-compression plan roughly *halves* your storage runway versus the compressed plan** (because the accepted-file average goes from ~15–20 MB to ~25–35 MB), and the 1 GB ceiling is reached far faster than the 20 GB Cloudflare-migration trigger in the PRD. **The binding constraint under this plan is the 1 GB free-tier *total storage* limit, not the 20 GB migration trigger.** The PRD's documented trigger (monthly cost > $25 OR storage > 20 GB) assumes you'd pay/migrate long before 20 GB — but on the free tier you hit a hard *wall* at 1 GB with no overage, uploads start failing, and that happens at ~25–35 videos. That mismatch is exactly what Q3 should fix.

**Q3 — Yes. Flag an explicit free-tier storage-exhaustion threshold in the PRD. The current migration trigger (20 GB) is unreachable on the free tier and gives false comfort.** Recommend PM add a Phase-1 operational trigger distinct from the Phase-2 migration trigger:
- A concrete **"free-tier storage watch" threshold at ~800 MB of total Supabase Storage** (80% of the 1 GB free cap, across all buckets) that triggers a conscious decision: either (a) upgrade to Supabase Pro (100 GB, 200 MB/file — but note this *crosses the $25 line the PRD defined as the Cloudflare-migration trigger*, so it's really a decision to defer Cloudflare by paying Supabase, same caveat as the prior reviews), or (b) execute the Cloudflare Stream migration early. 800 MB gives real lead time because at ~30 MB/video the last 200 MB is only ~6–7 more uploads of headroom — so 800 MB, not 950 MB, is the right alarm point.
- This belongs in the PRD/production.md because it reinterprets the §4/line 321 migration trigger for the free-tier reality. It is a Tech Lead finding → PM-logged change → Doc-Sync, not an ops note I can slip into production.md myself.

**Concerns** (must address before proceeding):
- **AC-031 and AC-169 are in direct conflict with this plan and will be left failing if the plan ships without revising them.** AC-031 mandates compression; AC-169 is defined as a *post-compression* guard. Both must be rewritten (AC-031 → "no client-side compression; upfront source-size gate at 45 MB"; AC-169 → the upfront source-size guard + new error copy) via PM → Doc-Sync before/with the code change. Shipping the code first would leave two Phase-1 ACs in a permanent fail state — the exact anti-pattern called out in the first 413 review (Option A shipped alone).
- **This contradicts three prior Tech Lead reviews that all concluded "compress-before-upload is the fix for AC-031."** That is fine — new information (the quality complaint, the repeated native-transcode fragility, and a concrete Phase-2 Stream plan) justifies the reversal — but the reversal must be *recorded as a deliberate decision*, because production.md line 127 ("recommended client-side compression library is `react-native-compressor`") and the Video Pipeline Conventions will now say the opposite of the code. Reconcile the convention (remove the compression-library line, state "Phase 1 uploads originals under a 45 MB source-size gate; no client-side compression; Cloudflare Stream transcode is the Phase-2 path") via PM → Doc-Sync. Do not let the code and production.md disagree.
- **The 45 MB gate + 60 s duration cap will reject a large share of legitimate 30–60 s iPhone clips (Q2).** This is a user-facing behavior change that QA must be able to verify and that users will hit often. PM should decide consciously whether that friction is acceptable for Phase 1, and the AC-030/US-004 "≤60 s" promise should be reconciled with the practical "~22 s at full HEVC bitrate" reality of the gate. At minimum the bilingual error copy must be clear and actionable (the proposed "請壓縮至 45MB 以下 / keep it under 45 MB" is reasonable, though "請修剪影片 / trim your video" may be more actionable than "compress," since users have no easy compress tool on-device).
- **Codec enforcement is now container-only (Q1).** With no transcode normalizing output, a `.mov` wrapping a non-H.264/HEVC/non-AAC codec would be stored and could be unplayable. Accept the "iPhone footage is always H.264/HEVC + AAC" assumption for Phase 1 and document it, or add a codec probe (Phase 2). Don't leave AC-035's codec requirement silently under-enforced without a recorded rationale.
- **HDR HEVC playback must be verified on a physical device, not the simulator** (Q1), before this is called done — the "plays correctly via AVPlayer, HDR preserved" claim is plausible but is exactly the kind of native-path assertion the project's conventions require device validation for.

**Recommendations** (advisory — human/PM decide; code → `engineer-mod-beta-video`, AC/convention → PM → Doc-Sync):
- **Adopt the no-compression plan, but sequence it as a PRD change, not a code patch.** Order: (1) PM revises AC-031 (no client-side compression; upfront 45 MB source-size gate), AC-169 (upfront source gate + new error copy), and adds the Q3 free-tier 800 MB watch threshold; (2) PM logs the Video Pipeline Convention reversal (remove the `react-native-compressor` compression line; Phase 1 = originals under a 45 MB gate, no compression; Phase 2 = Cloudflare Stream transcode); (3) Doc-Sync carries both into production.md + the MOD-005 spec; (4) `engineer-mod-beta-video` removes the compressor, moves the size gate upfront (fail-closed), simplifies the AC-036 overlay, and removes the three compressor references; (5) QA verifies accept-under-45 MB, reject-at/over-45 MB (with the new bilingual copy), fail-closed on unknown size, and HDR HEVC playback on a physical device.
- **Remove `react-native-compressor` entirely (not dormant)** — it's a native-build liability with no Phase-2 need once Stream handles transcode. Use `npx expo install` tooling to drop it; don't hand-edit `package.json`.
- **Keep the 45 MB decimal threshold and the fail-closed behavior** from the shipped guard — those are correct and hard-won. Only the *timing* (upfront, on source) and the *error copy* change.
- **Draft the Cloudflare Stream migration runbook before Phase 1 GA** (still an open §12/production.md line 321 risk). Under the no-compression plan you will hit the 1 GB wall sooner, so the runbook is more urgent, not less — the migration could be forced within the first weeks of a real single-gym launch.
- **Validate HDR HEVC inline + fullscreen playback on a physical iPhone** against `expo-video@57` `VideoView` before QA sign-off, specifically checking the inline-HDR-on-SDR-display tone-mapping look, not just "does it play."

**Approved** (sound as-is, no rethink needed):
- The **reject-at-the-gate architecture itself** — simpler than compression, zero device CPU/battery, removes the fragile native transcode path that caused three defects. It is a legitimate and arguably better Phase-1 choice than compression *given* the Phase-2 Stream plan absorbs the quality/HDR story server-side.
- **Cloudflare Stream as the Phase-2 destination** (upload original, server-side HDR→SDR tone-map + CDN) — already the PRD-named migration target; the plan is consistent with it, and removing client compression now means there's nothing to un-build when Stream arrives.
- **The 45 MB decimal source-size threshold** as the gate value (safe margin under the 50 MB free-tier bucket ceiling) and the **fail-closed** rule on unknown size — carry both forward from the shipped guard.
- **Storing originals as-is (no transcode)** — already the documented AC-035 / production.md line 127 intent for the stored artifact; the plan aligns with it.

**Proposed Shared Conventions** (for Doc-Sync to carry into production.md, only if the human/PM approve — these *supersede* the compression conventions from the three prior reviews below):
- **Phase 1 beta-video upload stores the original file as-is behind an upfront 45 MB (decimal) source-size gate — there is NO client-side compression.** The gate reads source size via `new ExpoFile(asset.uri).size` *before* upload and **fails closed** (unknown/0/unreadable size → reject with the bilingual too-large error, never upload). This supersedes the prior "client-side compression via `react-native-compressor`" convention; `react-native-compressor` is removed from the project. Accepted containers remain `.mp4`/`.mov` with H.264/HEVC + AAC (AC-035); iPhone camera/library footage satisfies this by construction, so Phase 1 enforces container-only (no client-side codec probe).
- **Client-side video compression and HDR→SDR tone-mapping are deferred to the Phase-2 Cloudflare Stream migration (server-side transcode), not done on the client.** Phase 1 trades the quality-loss/transcode-fragility risk of client compression for size-rejection friction at the 45 MB gate; Stream absorbs transcode, HDR tone-mapping, adaptive bitrate, and CDN when the migration fires.
- **Free-tier storage has a hard 1 GB total ceiling (shared across all buckets); a watch threshold at ~800 MB triggers the Supabase-Pro-vs-Cloudflare-Stream decision.** This is distinct from the PRD §4 Phase-2 migration trigger (monthly cost > US$25 OR storage > 20 GB), which is unreachable on the free tier — the free-tier wall at 1 GB (≈25–35 un-compressed videos) is the real Phase-1 constraint. Adopting Supabase Pro to get headroom is itself a crossing of the $25 migration-trigger line and must be a conscious, PM-logged decision.
- **Native video behavior (HDR HEVC playback, source-size reads) is verified on a physical iOS device before QA sign-off, not the simulator** — extends the existing "Native build gate before QA handoff" convention.

---

### Review — 2026-10-01 — change (MOD-005 beta-video upload: QUALITY brainstorm — the 2 Mbps/1280 config produces unusable beta, reconcile quality vs the 50 MB free-tier ceiling)

**Context**: Human requested a broad architectural brainstorm (options + a concrete phased recommendation, not a single answer) for the beta-video upload flow. The 413 defect is fixed; the live problem is now the opposite — the config chosen to guarantee files fit under 50 MB (`compressionMethod: 'manual', bitrate: 2_000_000, maxSize: 1280`) produces output too blurry to read hand/foot placement, which makes the beta useless for its one job. Advisory only — I read `BetaVideoUploader.tsx`, `beta-video-service.ts`, `production.md`, and the **installed** `react-native-compressor@1.16.0` native/TS sources. No source, migration, spec, or PRD files changed here. This review is a decision menu for the human + PM; nothing below is a directive to change code.

**Grounding — what the installed library can and cannot do (this constrains half the invocation's questions):**
- `BetaVideoUploader.tsx` lines 196–206 call `CompressorVideo.compress(uri, { compressionMethod: 'manual', bitrate: 2_000_000, maxSize: 1280 })`. `maxSize: 1280` caps the **longest** side at 1280 — for portrait 1080×1920 source that forces the output to **720×1280 (720p)**, not 1080p. So the app is *already* shipping 720p; the quality complaint is a bitrate complaint on a 720p frame, not a resolution one.
- **The library hardcodes H.264 on iOS and offers no HEVC option.** `node_modules/react-native-compressor/ios/Video/VideoMain.swift` line 264 sets `AVVideoCodecKey: AVVideoCodecType.h264` with `AVVideoProfileLevelH264HighAutoLevel`, unconditionally. The TS options type (`Video/index.d.ts`) exposes only `bitrate`, `maxSize`, `compressionMethod`, `minimumFileSizeForCompress`, `progressDivider` — **there is no codec parameter**. Audio is hardcoded AAC 128 kbps stereo 44.1 kHz. **Conclusion for the invocation's HEVC question: `react-native-compressor` does NOT support HEVC output. The "HEVC at half the bitrate" lever is unavailable without switching libraries or going server-side.** This is the single most important fact in this review.
- The library also exports `createVideoThumbnail` and `getVideoMetaData` helpers — relevant to the Phase-1 thumbnail placeholder and a quality-picker size preview, should those be pursued later.
- Math for a 60 s clip at 720p H.264 (keep this table handy — it bounds every "stay on free tier" option):
  - 2.0 Mbps (current) → ~15 MB. Safe under 50 MB, but visibly soft on a 720p climbing frame. **This is the current complaint.**
  - 3.5 Mbps (Instagram-Reels reference for a similar short portrait clip) → ~26 MB + ~1 MB audio ≈ **~27 MB**. Comfortably under the 45 MB guard / 50 MB bucket.
  - 5.0 Mbps → ~37.5 MB + ~1 MB ≈ **~38 MB**. Still under 45 MB, but thin headroom — a 60.0 s clip at 5 Mbps is close to the guard.
  - 6.0 Mbps → ~45 MB — **at the guard**; unsafe for a full-length clip. This is the practical ceiling at 50 MB/60 s.

**Analysis of each option family (honest trade-offs):**

**Quality options (client-side tuning) — this is where the Phase-1 win is.**
- *Bitrate target.* On a 720p frame, 2 Mbps is genuinely low for high-motion climbing footage (whole-body movement + texture of holds/wall is exactly the "high spatial + temporal detail" case H.264 handles worst at low bitrate). Instagram Reels ~3.5 Mbps and TikTok ~2–3 Mbps are the right reference class, and those are often 1080p. For **720p climbing beta, 3.5 Mbps is the sweet spot** — a 75% bitrate increase over today lands at ~27 MB (well within budget) and is where H.264 stops showing obvious blocking on motion. 5 Mbps is a defensible "high" tier if we add a picker, but eats the free-tier headroom.
- *HEVC.* Perceptually the right answer (same quality at ~half the bitrate, iPhones decode it natively), **but unavailable in the current library** (hardcoded H.264, shown above). Pursuing HEVC means either a different client library or server-side transcode — a Phase-2-scale decision, not a config tweak. Do not treat HEVC as an available knob for the immediate fix.
- *Resolution.* We're already at 720p via `maxSize: 1280`. 720p is the correct target for phone-screen beta — going to 1080p would roughly double the bytes for detail that's largely invisible on the small inline player and would blow the 50 MB budget. **Keep 720p; spend the recovered budget on bitrate, not pixels.**

**Storage-limit options.**
- *Supabase Pro ($25/mo, 100 GB, 200 MB/file).* Removes the ceiling entirely and is the cleanest single lever — but note the PRD's own migration trigger is "monthly cost > US$25 OR storage > 20 GB." Paying for Pro *is* crossing the $25 line that the PRD defined as the Cloudflare-migration trigger, so adopting Pro is really a decision to **defer the Cloudflare migration by paying Supabase instead** — a legitimate choice, but it should be made consciously against §4/line 321, not slipped in. For a single-city Phase-1 launch it is premature: compression tuning gets usable quality for $0.
- *Cloudflare R2 (keep Supabase Auth/RLS, move only storage).* Technically possible — R2 is S3-compatible and Auth/RLS stay on Supabase — but you lose Supabase Storage's RLS-integrated signed URLs and would hand-roll signing/authorization against R2 (presigned URLs minted by an Edge Function that checks the Supabase JWT). That's real migration complexity (new bucket, new signing path, the `getVideoSignedUrl`/`getThumbnailSignedUrl` functions rewritten, the Storage-RLS NFR re-satisfied on a non-Supabase store). Not worth it for Phase 1; it's a Phase-2 cost-optimization, and if you're moving video off Supabase anyway, **Cloudflare Stream (purpose-built) beats raw R2** for this use case.
- *Stay on free tier.* Entirely viable at the scale described. Max usable quality at 50 MB/60 s is ~5–6 Mbps H.264 720p (per the table), and 3.5 Mbps leaves comfortable headroom. The free tier is not the constraint on *quality* — the 2 Mbps config was simply over-conservative.

**Architecture options.**
- *Client-side only (current).* Simplest, zero new infra, one proven upload path (`File.bytes()` → `Uint8Array` → `.upload()`). Cost: the user waits through a compress phase (already surfaced via the AC-036 overlay) and spends device battery. For ≤60 s clips on an iPhone 12+ (the NFR floor) this is seconds, not minutes — acceptable for Phase 1.
- *Upload original → server-side transcode (Edge Function / Mux / Bunny).* Best quality ceiling and no client battery cost, but requires storing the raw 100–120 MB original first (which **needs** Pro or an external store — you can't stage a 111 MB raw file in the 50 MB free bucket), plus an async "processing" state the UI doesn't have today, plus new infra. This is a scale-stage architecture, not a Phase-1 fix. Supabase Edge Functions specifically are a poor host for video transcode (CPU/time limits) — this path really means Mux/Bunny/Cloudflare Stream.
- *Dedicated video platform (Mux / Cloudflare Stream).* The correct Phase-2 destination — it's already named in the PRD as the migration target (Cloudflare Stream). It solves transcode + adaptive streaming + CDN + HEVC in one move. Over-engineered for a single-gym-city launch; adopting it now front-loads cost and integration risk before there's usage to justify it.
- *TUS / chunked resumable.* Already analyzed and rejected for Phase 1 in the two prior reviews below — it does not reduce stored bytes (irrelevant to the quality problem), and it requires departing from the `File.bytes()`/`Uint8Array` upload convention (the `Response.blob()` ban exists precisely because that path corrupted uploads). Nothing here changes that. Out of scope.

**User-experience options.**
- *In-app trimmer.* Genuinely useful for this domain — a 15 s crux clip at high bitrate is both smaller AND better beta than a soft 60 s clip. But it adds a native dependency + a build/QA cycle, and AC-030 already caps duration at 60 s. **Defer to Phase 2**; not needed to fix the immediate quality problem.
- *Quality picker (High/Medium/Low + size preview).* Low effort (it's just swapping the `bitrate` constant per selection, and `getVideoMetaData`/`getFileSize` can show a pre-upload size), and it hands the quality/size trade-off to the user. Reasonable **Phase-1.5** polish, but it adds UI/i18n/QA surface. For the immediate fix, a single well-chosen default beats a picker.
- *Duration-based bitrate (shorter → higher bitrate).* This is the quietly clever option and it fits the 50 MB ceiling *perfectly*, because the constraint is total bytes = bitrate × duration. A tiered map keeps every clip near the quality ceiling while never exceeding budget: e.g. **≤15 s → 8 Mbps, ≤30 s → 6 Mbps, ≤45 s → 4.5 Mbps, ≤60 s → 3.5 Mbps** (each lands roughly 15–20 MB, all safely under 45 MB). It needs no new UI, no new dependency, and no user decision — it's a pure function of the duration the code *already measures* (`asset.duration`, used for the AC-030 check). The only cost is a few lines mapping duration → bitrate before the `compress()` call. Strongest UX option for Phase 1.

**Concerns** (must address before proceeding):
- **Any bitrate change must stay inside the AC-169 budget, and the guard must remain the hard backstop.** The recommended 3.5 Mbps (or the duration-tiered map, whose worst case is the ≤60 s/3.5 Mbps bucket) is designed to land ~27 MB, well under the 45 MB guard — but the guard (now correctly failing *closed* after the prior fix) must stay as the catch for pathological inputs. Do not raise the bitrate to a level (≥6 Mbps for a full 60 s clip) where a legitimate upload routinely hits the guard; that would turn the quality fix into a new rejection bug.
- **Decide the Supabase-Pro-vs-stay-free question explicitly against the PRD migration trigger, not implicitly.** Paying for Pro is crossing the $25 line the PRD defined as the Cloudflare-migration trigger. If the team wants the headroom, that's fine — but it's a PM-logged decision that reinterprets §4/line 321, not an ops toggle. The recommendation below does **not** require Pro.
- **HEVC is off the table with the current library — don't let it leak into the spec as an option.** The convention and any spec text must not imply HEVC output is achievable client-side today; it is not (hardcoded H.264). If HEVC is wanted, it's a library-swap or server-transcode decision for Phase 2, routed through PM.
- **A bitrate change is implementation under existing AC-031, but a duration-tiered bitrate MAP is a behavior worth pinning in an AC so QA can verify it.** Bumping a single constant 2→3.5 Mbps is pure implementation (route to `engineer-mod-beta-video`, no PRD change). But if the team adopts the duration-tiered map or a quality picker, that is new, user-observable behavior with specific thresholds QA must test — that should be a small new AC under US-004 (PM → Doc-Sync), not an undocumented engineering choice.

**Recommendations** (advisory — the human + PM decide; implementation routes to `engineer-mod-beta-video`):

- **PHASE 1 — DO NOW (zero new infra, zero new dependency, stays on free tier):**
  1. **Raise the bitrate from 2 Mbps to 3.5 Mbps**, keep `maxSize: 1280` (720p). This is a one-constant change in `BetaVideoUploader.tsx` that lands a 60 s clip at ~27 MB — safely inside the 45 MB guard and 50 MB bucket — and moves quality into the Instagram-Reels class. This alone likely resolves the "unusable beta" complaint. Pure implementation under AC-031; no PRD change.
  2. **Strongly consider the duration-tiered bitrate map instead of a flat 3.5 Mbps** (≤15 s → 8, ≤30 s → 6, ≤45 s → 4.5, ≤60 s → 3.5 Mbps). It's only marginally more code, needs no UI, exploits the budget optimally (short crux clips — the common case for beta — come out near-pristine), and never threatens the guard. If adopted, PM should pin the thresholds in a small AC under US-004 so QA can verify them.
  3. **Verify output quality AND size on a physical iPhone, not the Simulator** — per the existing "verify compression on device" convention (prior review). The simulator's software AVFoundation path is unreliable for exactly this measurement. Confirm a real 54 s HEVC-source clip (the `IMG_7964.mov` test file) comes out visibly readable and under the guard at the new bitrate.
  4. **Do NOT buy Supabase Pro, adopt R2/Stream/Mux, add TUS, add a trimmer, or switch compression libraries for the immediate fix.** None are needed to get usable quality at this scale; each adds cost/infra/risk the single-city launch doesn't warrant.

- **PHASE 2 — PLAN FOR (revisit when the PRD trigger fires: monthly video cost > US$25 OR storage > 20 GB, or when quality demands outgrow H.264 720p):**
  1. **Cloudflare Stream** is the destination already named in the PRD — it brings HEVC/adaptive bitrate/CDN/transcode and removes the per-file ceiling. Prefer it over raw R2 (which would make you hand-roll signing and still owe you a transcode story). Draft the migration runbook (still an open §12 risk) *before* GA so the cutover isn't a fire drill.
  2. **Server-side transcode (via Stream/Mux/Bunny, not Supabase Edge Functions)** unlocks "upload original, best quality, no client battery cost" with an async processing state — but only once there's an external store able to hold the raw original, which Phase 2 infra provides anyway.
  3. **Quality picker and/or in-app trimmer** as UX polish once there's usage data showing users want the control. The library already exports `getVideoMetaData`/`getFileSize` to drive a size preview.
  4. **HEVC** arrives naturally with Cloudflare Stream (server-side), which is the clean way to get it — not by swapping the client compressor.

**Approved** (nothing here needs rethinking):
- The overall Phase-1 architecture — compress-before-upload on the client, single-shot `File.bytes()` → `Uint8Array` → `.upload()`, AC-169 fail-closed size guard — remains correct. The quality problem is a *tuning* problem inside this architecture, not an architecture problem.
- Staying on 720p (`maxSize: 1280`). It's the right resolution for phone-screen beta; the fix is bitrate, not pixels.
- Staying on the Supabase free tier for Phase 1. The 50 MB ceiling is not the binding constraint on quality once the bitrate is chosen sensibly.
- The AC-169 guard (fail-closed) as the hard backstop behind whatever bitrate is chosen.

**Proposed Shared Conventions** (for Doc-Sync to carry into production.md — only if the human/PM approve; these refine, not replace, the existing Video Pipeline Conventions):
- **Beta-video client compression targets 720p H.264 (`maxSize: 1280`) at a bitrate chosen to maximize readable quality while staying under the AC-169 size budget — not the minimum bitrate that fits.** The Phase-1 target is ≥3.5 Mbps (Instagram-Reels class) for a full-length clip; the budget headroom under 50 MB/60 s belongs to quality, since blurry beta fails the feature's purpose. If a duration-tiered bitrate is adopted, shorter clips get proportionally higher bitrate (bytes = bitrate × duration is the governing constraint), and the tier thresholds are pinned in an AC so QA can verify them.
- **HEVC/H.265 output is not available via `react-native-compressor` (iOS output is hardcoded H.264); any HEVC requirement is a Phase-2 server-side-transcode or library-swap decision, routed through PM — it is not a client-side config knob.** Specs and code comments must not imply client-side HEVC is achievable today.
- **Supabase Pro / Cloudflare R2 / Cloudflare Stream / Mux / server-side transcode / TUS are explicitly Phase-2 levers for the beta-video pipeline, gated on the PRD §4 migration trigger (monthly cost > US$25 OR storage > 20 GB). Phase 1 stays on the Supabase free tier with client-side compression tuned for quality.** Adopting Supabase Pro is itself a crossing of the $25 migration-trigger line and must be a conscious, PM-logged decision rather than an ops toggle.
